In contrast, teleparallelism is fully equivalent to the Hilbert-Einstein action theory
[
63
]
. This is another class of gravity theories in which the gravity is represented by the torsion, not by curvature (which vanishes identically).
On the other hand, the Palatini formulation of GR, a first-order formulation of the HE action, is closely related to the Einstein-Cartan theory. In this work, we shall restrict ourselves to GR described by the HE action (with the Levi-Civita connection).

Using the Euler-Lagrange equations, we obtain from Eq. (
112
) the Einstein equations
[
43
]
:

(120)

R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}R=\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2}T_{\mu\nu},

where
R=R_{\mu\nu}g^{\mu\nu}
and
T_{\mu\nu}
is the energy-momentum tensor.

Instead of the metric
g_{\mu\nu}
, one can use

(121)

h^{\mu\nu}\equiv\sqrt{-g}g^{\mu\nu},

which is convenient for perturbative expansions of the metric around the Minkowski background. The Hilbert-Einstein action becomes

(122)

S_{\text{HE}}=\int\mathop{d^{D}x}{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{HE}},

where
[
64
]

(123)

{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{HE}}=-\frac{1}{4\kappa^{2}}\left(h^{\rho\sigma}h_{\lambda\alpha}h_{\kappa\tau}h^{\alpha\kappa}{}_{,\rho}h^{\lambda\tau}{}_{,\sigma}-\frac{1}{D-2}h^{\rho\sigma}h_{\alpha\kappa}h_{\lambda\tau}h^{\alpha\kappa}{}_{,\rho}h^{\lambda\tau}{}_{,\sigma}-2h_{\alpha\tau}h^{\alpha\kappa}{}_{,\rho}h^{\rho\tau}{}_{,\kappa}\right).

This Lagrangian is invariant under the gauge transformation
[
64
]

(124)

\delta h^{\mu\nu}=\kappa[h^{\mu\rho}\partial_{\rho}\theta^{\nu}+h^{\nu\rho}\partial_{\rho}\theta^{\mu}-\partial_{\rho}(h^{\mu\nu}\theta^{\rho})].

Since we are interested in investigating gravity in a perturbative regime, we introduce a background metric. For simplicity, we choose a flat background, then
h^{\mu\nu}
in this background reads

(125)

h^{\mu\nu}(x)=\eta^{\mu\nu}+\kappa\phi^{\mu\nu}(x).

The fluctuations of
h^{\mu\nu}
around the background are the graviton field, which is the spin-2 gauge field responsible for mediating gravitational interactions. When expanded in terms of
\phi^{\mu\nu}
, the quantization of the HE action (
123
) can be carried out using the standard quantization procedure, previously applied in YM theory.

6.1
Quantization

Here, we proceed with the quantization of the HE action. For more details, the reader is referred to
[
64
, Appendix B]
.

The procedure is analogous to the quantization of the YM theory in section
4.3
. First, we introduce a gauge-fixing term such as

(126)

{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{gf}}(\phi)=-\frac{1}{2\alpha\kappa^{2}}(\partial_{\mu}h^{\mu\nu})^{2}=-\frac{1}{2\alpha}(\partial_{\mu}\phi^{\mu\nu})^{2}

in which we used that
\partial_{\mu}h^{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}(\eta^{\mu\nu}+\kappa\phi^{\mu\nu})=\kappa\partial_{\mu}\phi^{\mu\nu}
. This is the analog of (
31
) for gravity.
For
\alpha=1
, we have the so-called DeDonder gauge, which simplifies considerably any perturbative computation.

Using Eq. (
126
), which corresponds to the gauge condition
\partial_{\mu}\phi^{\mu\nu}=0
, will lead to the introduction of the corresponding FP ghost fields
d_{\nu}
,
\bar{d}_{\mu}
. The ghost action reads
[
13
]

(127)

{\mathcal{L}}_{\textrm{gh}}(\phi)=\bar{d}_{\mu}\left\{\partial^{2}\eta^{\mu\nu}+\kappa\left[(\phi^{\rho\sigma}_{,\rho})\partial_{\sigma}\eta^{\mu\nu}-(\phi^{\rho\mu}_{,\rho})\partial^{\nu}+\phi^{\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\sigma}\eta^{\mu\nu}-(\partial_{\rho}\partial^{\nu}\phi^{\rho\mu})\right]\right\}d_{\nu}.

Thus, the effective action of second-order gravity (SOGR) is

(128)

{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{eff}}^{\text{II}}={\mathcal{L}}_{\text{HE}}+{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{gf}}(\phi)+{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{gh}}(\phi).

The path integral quantization follows directly

(129)

Z_{\text{HE}}[j]=\int\mathop{\mathcal{D}\phi^{\mu\nu}}\mathop{\mathcal{D}\bar{d}_{\mu}}\mathop{\mathcal{D}d_{\nu}}\exp{i\left[S_{\text{HE}}+\int\mathop{d^{D}x}\left({\mathcal{L}}_{\text{gf}}(\phi)+{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{gh}}(\phi)+{j}_{\mu\nu}\phi^{\mu\nu}\right)\right]},

where
S_{\text{HE}}
is given by Eq. (
122
) with
h^{\mu\nu}=\eta^{\mu\nu}+\kappa\phi^{\mu\nu}
.

On the other hand, we also need
h_{\mu\nu}=g_{\mu\nu}/\sqrt{-g}=(h^{\mu\nu})^{-1}
. The inverse of Eq. (
125
) yields the following power series:

(130)

h_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}-\kappa\phi_{\mu\nu}+\kappa^{2}\phi_{\mu\rho}\phi_{\nu\rho}+\mathcal{O}(\kappa^{3}).

This implies that the HE action in Eq. (
123
) shall lead to infinite momentum-dependent interaction vertices. This is one of the reasons for the non-renormalizability of quantum gravity. Besides that, it considerably complicates the computation of diagrams. The Feynman rules of the SOGR used in this work are presented in appendix
10
.

6.2
BRST symmetry

The BRST symmetry of the action in Eq. (
129
) reads

(131a)

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}\phi^{\mu\nu}=h^{\mu\rho}\partial_{\rho}d^{\nu}+h^{\nu\rho}\partial_{\rho}d^{\mu}-\partial_{\rho}(h^{\mu\nu}d^{\rho}),

(131b)

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}d^{\mu}=\kappa d^{\rho}d^{\mu}_{,\rho},

(131c)

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}\bar{d}^{\mu}=B^{\mu}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{s}B^{\mu}=0,

where
B^{\mu}
is a Nakanishi-Lautrup field
[
65
,
66
]
. The Nakanishi-Lautrup field is necessary to ensure the off-shell nilpotency of the BRST symmetry.

As we have seen in Section
5.6
, we obtain the Slavnov-Taylor identities by extending the source term. In the SOGR, the extended source term is

(132)

j_{\mu\nu}\phi^{\mu\nu}+i(\bar{\eta}_{\mu}d^{\mu}-\bar{d}^{\mu}\eta_{\mu})+j_{\mu}B^{\mu}+K_{\mu\nu}\left[h^{\mu\rho}\partial_{\rho}d^{\nu}+h^{\nu\rho}\partial_{\rho}d^{\mu}-\partial_{\rho}(h^{\mu\nu}d^{\rho})\right]+\kappa K_{\mu}d^{\rho}d^{\mu}_{,\rho}.

The corresponding Zinn-Justin master equation is given by

(133)

\int\mathop{dx}\left(\frac{\delta_{R}\Gamma}{\delta\phi^{\mu\nu}}\frac{\delta_{L}\Gamma}{\delta K_{\mu\nu}}+\frac{\delta_{R}\Gamma}{\delta d^{\mu}}\frac{\delta_{L}\Gamma}{\delta K_{\mu}}+\frac{\delta_{R}\Gamma}{\delta\bar{d}^{\mu}}\frac{\delta_{L}\Gamma}{\delta j_{\mu}}\right)=0,

which is based on the invariance of the generating functional (
129
) under the SOGR BRST symmetry. The Slavnov-Taylor identities of the second-order formulation of gravity are derived by taking functional derivatives of this master equation.

7
Hilbert-Palatini action

In this section, we examine the first-order formulation of gravity (FOGR) also known as the Hilbert-Palatini (HP) formalism
[
67
]
. In the standard formalism, the (Levi-Civita) connection
\Gamma(g^{\mu\nu})
is a metric-dependent variable whose components are given by the Christoffel symbol. However, in the HP formalism, metric and the (affine) connection are treated as independent fields. The affine connection plays a similar role to the auxiliary field
\mathcal{F}
in the FOYM formulation.

As we have done in the SOGR, we replace
g^{\mu\nu}
by
h^{\mu\nu}
and the connection
\Gamma
by
[
13
]

(134)

\mathbb{G}_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}\equiv\mathchoice{\Gamma^{{{\lambda}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu\nu}}}}{\Gamma^{{{\lambda}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu\nu}}}}{\Gamma^{{{\lambda}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu\nu}}}}{\Gamma^{{{\lambda}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu\nu}}}}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\delta_{\mu}^{\lambda}\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\nu\alpha}+\delta_{\nu}^{\lambda}\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\alpha}\right).

Replacing
\mathbb{G}_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}
with the auxiliary field
G_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}
, we arrive at the FOGR Lagrangian

(135)

{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{HP}}(h)=\frac{h^{\mu\nu}}{\kappa^{2}}\left(G^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu,\lambda}+\frac{1}{D-1}G^{\lambda}_{\mu\lambda}G^{\sigma}_{\nu\sigma}-G^{\lambda}_{\mu\sigma}G^{\sigma}_{\nu\lambda}\right).

The field
G^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}
is an auxiliary field analogous to
\mathcal{F}_{\mu\nu}^{a}
, and
\mathbb{G}_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}
in Eq. (
134
) is its classical value. The FOGR Lagrangian is invariant under the following gauge transformation
[
13
]

(136)

\displaystyle\delta h^{\mu\nu}={}

\displaystyle\kappa\left[h^{\mu\rho}\partial_{\rho}\theta^{\nu}+h^{\nu\rho}\partial_{\rho}\theta^{\mu}-\partial_{\rho}(h^{\mu\nu}\theta^{\rho})\right]

and

\displaystyle\delta{G}{{}^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}}={}

\displaystyle\kappa\bigg[-\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\theta^{\lambda}+\frac{1}{2}(\delta_{\mu}^{\lambda}\partial_{\nu}+\delta_{\nu}^{\lambda}\partial_{\mu})\partial_{\rho}\theta^{\rho}-\theta^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}{G}{{}^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}}

(137)

\displaystyle\quad+{G}{{}^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}}\partial_{\rho}\theta^{\lambda}-({G}{{}^{\lambda}_{\mu\rho}}\partial_{\nu}+{G}{{}^{\lambda}_{\nu\rho}}\partial_{\mu})\theta^{\rho}\bigg].

It is important to note that
\mathcal{F}_{\mu\nu}^{a}
is associated with the curvature in the YM theory
F_{\mu\nu}^{a}
, while
G^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}
is associated with the Levi-Civita connection
\Gamma^{\lambda}{}_{\mu\nu}
.
In pure theories, without matter fields, this distinction is not relevant, and the first-order formulation of gravity is quite analogous to the FOYM. However, the coupling with matter fields breaks this parallel since the minimal coupling of matter fields is generally related to the connection. Thus, in the first-order formulation of gravity, the coupling is associated with the auxiliary field
G^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}
. This contrasts with the FOYM, where matter couples to the gauge field (the connection
A_{\mu}^{a}
) rather than the auxiliary field, which is associated with the curvature
F_{\mu\nu}^{a}
.

We can rewrite Eq. (
135
) as

(138)

\mathcal{L}_{\textrm{HP}}=\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}G^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}\mathchoice{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}(h)G^{\rho}_{\pi\tau}-\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}G^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}h^{\mu\nu}{}_{,\lambda},

where

(139)

\begin{split}\mathchoice{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\sigma}\kern 3.73024pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\sigma}\kern 3.73024pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\sigma}\kern 3.48792pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\sigma}\kern 3.48792pt}}}(h)&=\frac{1}{2}\Big[\frac{1}{D-1}\left(\delta^{\nu}_{\lambda}\delta^{\tau}_{\sigma}h^{\mu\pi}+\delta^{\mu}_{\lambda}\delta^{\tau}_{\sigma}h^{\nu\pi}+\delta^{\nu}_{\lambda}\delta^{\pi}_{\sigma}h^{\mu\tau}+\delta^{\mu}_{\lambda}\delta^{\pi}_{\sigma}h^{\nu\tau}\right)\\
&\qquad\quad-\left(\delta^{\tau}_{\lambda}\delta^{\nu}_{\sigma}h^{\mu\pi}+\delta^{\tau}_{\lambda}\delta^{\mu}_{\sigma}h^{\nu\pi}+\delta^{\pi}_{\lambda}\delta^{\nu}_{\sigma}h^{\mu\tau}+\delta^{\pi}_{\lambda}\delta^{\mu}_{\sigma}h^{\nu\tau}\right)\Big].\end{split}

Note that, Eq. (
138
) and the FOYM Lagrangian have a similar structure. Expanding the metric
h^{\mu\nu}
around a flat background, as in Eq. (
125
), Eq. (
138
) becomes

(140)

\mathcal{L}_{\text{HP}}=\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}G^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}\mathchoice{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}(\eta+\kappa\phi)G^{\rho}_{\pi\tau}-\frac{1}{\kappa}G^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}\phi^{\mu\nu}{}_{,\lambda}.

We find that, remarkably, we have a single cubic interaction
(GG\phi)
which does not depend on momentum, but now we have the mixed propagators
\langle 0|TG{}^{\lambda}{}_{\mu\nu}\phi^{\alpha\beta}|0\rangle_{\text{free}}
and
\langle 0|T\phi^{\alpha\beta}G{}^{\lambda}{}_{\mu\nu}|0\rangle_{\text{free}}
, and the propagator of the auxiliary field
\langle 0|TG{}^{\lambda}{}_{\mu\nu}G{}^{\rho}{}_{\pi\tau}|0\rangle_{\text{free}}
to compensate it. Nevertheless, the perturbation theory in the FOGR formalism is simplified considerably since infinite vertices are condensed in a finite number of components.

The Euler-Lagrange equations reads

(141a)

G^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}=\mathbb{G}^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}=\mathchoice{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}(h)h^{\pi\tau}{}_{,\rho},

(141b)

\frac{1}{2}{G}{{}^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}}{G}{{}^{\rho}_{\pi\tau}}\mathchoice{\mathcal{M}^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt{\alpha\beta}}}}{\mathcal{M}^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt{\alpha\beta}}}}{\mathcal{M}^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt{\alpha\beta}}}}{\mathcal{M}^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt{\alpha\beta}}}}+{G}{{}^{\lambda}_{\alpha\beta,\lambda}}=0;

where
\mathbb{G}^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}
denotes the classical value of the auxiliary field
G^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}
,

(142)

\mathchoice{\mathcal{M}^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt{\alpha\beta}}}}{\mathcal{M}^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt{\alpha\beta}}}}{\mathcal{M}^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt{\alpha\beta}}}}{\mathcal{M}^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt{\alpha\beta}}}}\equiv\frac{\partial\mathchoice{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\rho}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\sigma}\kern 4.19136pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\rho}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\sigma}\kern 4.19136pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\rho}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\sigma}\kern 3.71011pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\rho}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\sigma}\kern 3.71011pt}}}(h)}{\partial h^{\alpha\beta}},

and

(143)

\begin{split}\mathchoice{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}(h)={}&-\frac{1}{2(D-2)}h^{\lambda\rho}h_{\mu\nu}h_{\pi\tau}+\frac{1}{4}h^{\lambda\rho}\left(h_{\pi\mu}h_{\tau\nu}+h_{\pi\nu}h_{\tau\mu}\right)\\
&-\frac{1}{4}\left(h_{\tau\mu}\delta_{\nu}^{\rho}\delta_{\pi}^{\lambda}+h_{\pi\mu}\delta_{\nu}^{\rho}\delta_{\tau}^{\lambda}+h_{\tau\nu}\delta_{\mu}^{\rho}\delta_{\pi}^{\lambda}+h_{\pi\nu}\delta_{\mu}^{\rho}\delta_{\tau}^{\lambda}\right).\end{split}

This inverse satisfies
\mathchoice{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}\mathchoice{M^{{{\pi\tau}{\alpha\beta}}}_{{{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt{\gamma}\kern 5.53265pt}}}{M^{{{\pi\tau}{\alpha\beta}}}_{{{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt{\gamma}\kern 5.53265pt}}}{M^{{{\pi\tau}{\alpha\beta}}}_{{{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt{\gamma}\kern 4.78525pt}}}{M^{{{\pi\tau}{\alpha\beta}}}_{{{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt{\gamma}\kern 4.78525pt}}}=(\delta_{\mu}^{\alpha}\delta_{\nu}^{\beta}+\delta_{\mu}^{\beta}\delta_{\nu}^{\alpha})\delta_{\gamma}^{\lambda}/2
.

The Einstein-Hilbert equations are obtained by substituting Eq. (
141a
) in Eq. (
141b
):

(144)

\frac{1}{2}\mathchoice{h^{{{\mu\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.37271pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.37271pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.21945pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.01389pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{,\lambda}}}}{h^{{{\mu\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.37271pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.37271pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.21945pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.01389pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{,\lambda}}}}{h^{{{\mu\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.37271pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.37271pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.21945pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.01389pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{,\lambda}}}}{h^{{{\mu\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.37271pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.37271pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.21945pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.01389pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{,\lambda}}}}\mathchoice{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}(h)\mathchoice{\mathcal{M}^{{{\pi\tau}{\delta\sigma}}}_{{{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt{\gamma}\kern 4.06816pt{\alpha\beta}}}}{\mathcal{M}^{{{\pi\tau}{\delta\sigma}}}_{{{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt{\gamma}\kern 4.06816pt{\alpha\beta}}}}{\mathcal{M}^{{{\pi\tau}{\delta\sigma}}}_{{{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt{\gamma}\kern 3.6122pt{\alpha\beta}}}}{\mathcal{M}^{{{\pi\tau}{\delta\sigma}}}_{{{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt{\gamma}\kern 3.6122pt{\alpha\beta}}}}\mathchoice{(M^{-1})^{{{\gamma}\kern 4.06816pt{\chi}\kern 3.68013pt}}_{{{\delta\sigma}{\xi\omega}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\gamma}\kern 4.06816pt{\chi}\kern 3.68013pt}}_{{{\delta\sigma}{\xi\omega}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\gamma}\kern 3.6122pt{\chi}\kern 3.44215pt}}_{{{\delta\sigma}{\xi\omega}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\gamma}\kern 3.6122pt{\chi}\kern 3.44215pt}}_{{{\delta\sigma}{\xi\omega}}}}(h)h^{\xi\omega}{}_{,\chi}+\mathchoice{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.9901pt{\rho}\kern 4.72261pt}}_{{{\alpha\beta}{\mu\nu}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.9901pt{\rho}\kern 4.72261pt}}_{{{\alpha\beta}{\mu\nu}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.31833pt{\rho}\kern 4.17888pt}}_{{{\alpha\beta}{\mu\nu}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.31833pt{\rho}\kern 4.17888pt}}_{{{\alpha\beta}{\mu\nu}}}}(h)h^{\mu\nu}{}_{,\rho\lambda}=0,

which is equal to

(145)

\mathchoice{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.9901pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\alpha\beta}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.9901pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\alpha\beta}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.31833pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\alpha\beta}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.31833pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\alpha\beta}{\pi\tau}}}}(h)\mathchoice{M^{{{\pi\tau}{\delta\sigma}}}_{{{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt{\gamma}\kern 4.06816pt{,\lambda}}}}{M^{{{\pi\tau}{\delta\sigma}}}_{{{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt{\gamma}\kern 4.06816pt{,\lambda}}}}{M^{{{\pi\tau}{\delta\sigma}}}_{{{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt{\gamma}\kern 3.6122pt{,\lambda}}}}{M^{{{\pi\tau}{\delta\sigma}}}_{{{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt{\gamma}\kern 3.6122pt{,\lambda}}}}(h)\mathchoice{(M^{-1})^{{{\gamma}\kern 4.06816pt{\chi}\kern 3.68013pt}}_{{{\delta\sigma}{\xi\omega}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\gamma}\kern 4.06816pt{\chi}\kern 3.68013pt}}_{{{\delta\sigma}{\xi\omega}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\gamma}\kern 3.6122pt{\chi}\kern 3.44215pt}}_{{{\delta\sigma}{\xi\omega}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\gamma}\kern 3.6122pt{\chi}\kern 3.44215pt}}_{{{\delta\sigma}{\xi\omega}}}}(h)h^{\xi\omega}{}_{,\chi}=0.

Since it is pure gravity, the right-hand side (energy-momentum tensor) vanishes.

Moreover, using the classical equation of motion in Eq. (
141a
) in the FOGR Lagrangian Eq. (
138
) to solve the auxiliary field
G^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}
yields

(146)

{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{HE}}=-\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}h^{\mu\nu}{}_{,\lambda}\mathchoice{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}(h)h^{\pi\tau}{}_{,\rho},

which is equal to the SOGR Lagrangian given in Eq. (
123
). This demonstrates the classical equivalence between both formulations of gravity.

7.1
Quantization

We can find the appropriate generating functional of the FOGR formalism from the SOGR generating functional. This guarantees the quantum equivalence between these formulations. Besides that, we show that in the first-order formulation, second-class constraints arise. These constraints are treated appropriately within the FS procedure. Meanwhile, the second-order formulation of the gauge theories investigated in this work can be quantized by the standard FP procedure.

In the last chapter, we have shown that the Senjanović determinant in the FOYM is field-independent and can be absorbed by the normalization factor. This is a common characteristic of the Senjanović determinant. However, in certain systems it is not trivial
[
68
]
. In the FOGR theory, we find a field-dependent determinant factor, which can be interpreted as the Senjanović determinant.

Even though a trivial Senjanović determinant does not yield any significant contribution at the perturbative level, it may be relevant in certain scenarios. For instance, in quantum field theory at finite temperature, trivial ghosts lead to relevant contributions in the computation of the free energy
[
69
]
. We can understand this by the following reasoning. The free energy is proportional to the number of physical degrees of freedom of the theory. Therefore, even the trivial non-interacting ghosts have to be taken into account in order to cancel the unphysical degrees of freedom coming from the gauge field. Thus, we can assume that ghosts may be counted as a negative number of degrees of freedom and need to be accounted for.

For example, in four-dimensional QED, the gauge field has
4
components, although only
2
are physical (transversal components). In the path integral formalism, the FP ghosts
c
and
\bar{c}
are free fields, that is, they are trivial ghosts. Thus, as we have seen, four-dimensional QED should have
N_{A}-N_{c}-N_{\bar{c}}=4-2=2
degrees of freedom which is exactly the two possible polarization of the photon, the correct number of physical degrees of freedom of QED. This shows the relevance of finding the appropriate path integral, even when we only have trivial ghosts.

The standard approach would find all the constraints of the FOGR formalism to compute the Senjanović determinant, but this would lead to a non-covariant path integral
[
8
,
70
]
. A covariant canonical analysis appeared recently in
[
71
]
(and references within). However, the second-class constraints are solved. Since the second-class constraints are reducible, similar to the second-class in the FOYM theory, it is easier to solve them. A complete analysis, which does not solve the second-class constraints, such as done in
[
8
]
has the downside of not being manifestly covariant.

We can avoid these issues by using the procedure proposed in section
5.5
.
We start the procedure by inserting the factor

(147)

1=\int\mathop{\mathcal{D}G^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}}\Delta^{1/2}(h,J)\exp i\int\mathop{d^{D}x}\left(\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}G^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}\mathchoice{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}(h)G^{\rho}_{\pi\tau}+\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2}J_{\lambda}^{\mu\nu}\mathchoice{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}J_{\rho}^{\pi\tau}\right)

into the SOGR generating functional

(148)

Z_{\text{HE}}[j]=N\int\mathop{\mathcal{D}h^{\mu\nu}}\mathop{\mathcal{D}\bar{d}_{\mu}}\mathop{\mathcal{D}d_{\nu}}\exp{i\int\mathop{d^{D}x}\left({\mathcal{L}}_{\text{eff}}^{\text{II}}(h)+{j}_{\mu\nu}h^{\mu\nu}+J^{\lambda}{}_{\mu\nu}\mathbb{G}_{\lambda}^{\mu\nu}(h)\right)}

with an additional source for the classical value of the auxiliary field
\mathbb{G}^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}(h)
(see Eq. (
141a
)).

Then, we redefine the auxiliary field

(149)

G^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}\to G^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}-\mathbb{G}^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}+\kappa^{2}\mathchoice{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}(h)J_{\rho}{}^{\pi\tau}

yielding

(150)

N\int\mathop{\mathcal{D}\mu_{\text{I}}}\exp i\int\mathop{d^{D}x}\left({\mathcal{L}}_{\text{eff}}^{\text{I}}+J^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}G_{\lambda}^{\mu\nu}+\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2}J_{\lambda}^{\mu\nu}\mathchoice{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}(h)J_{\rho}^{\pi\tau}+j_{\mu\nu}h^{\mu\nu}\right),

where
\mathop{\mathcal{D}\mu_{\text{I}}}=\mathop{\mathcal{D}h^{\mu\nu}}\mathop{\mathcal{D}G^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}}\mathop{\mathcal{D}\bar{d}_{\mu}}\mathop{\mathcal{D}d_{\nu}}\Delta^{1/2}(h,J)
and

(151)

\mathcal{L}_{\text{eff}}^{\text{I}}=\mathcal{L}_{\text{HP}}(h)+\mathcal{L}_{\text{gf}}(h)+\mathcal{L}_{\text{gh}}(h)

denotes the effective first-order gravity Lagrangian.

Now, we need to determine
\Delta^{1/2}(h,J)
. For this, we integrate the auxiliary field in Eq. (
147
) arriving at

(152)

\Delta^{1/2}(h,J)=|\det\mathchoice{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}(h)|^{1/2}\exp i\int\mathop{d^{D}x}\left(-\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2}J_{\lambda}^{\mu\nu}\mathchoice{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}(h)J_{\rho}^{\pi\tau}\right).

Using Eq. (
152
), we obtain the resulting generating functional

(153)

Z_{\text{HE}}[j,J]=N\int\mathop{\mathcal{D}\mu_{\text{I}}^{\prime}}\exp i\int\mathop{d^{D}x}\left({\mathcal{L}}_{\text{eff}}^{\text{I}}+J^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}G_{\lambda}^{\mu\nu}+j_{\mu\nu}h^{\mu\nu}+\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2}J_{\lambda}^{\mu\nu}\mathchoice{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}(h)J_{\rho}^{\pi\tau}\right),

where

(154)

\mathop{\mathcal{D}\mu_{\text{I}}^{\prime}}=\mathop{\mathcal{D}h^{\mu\nu}}\mathop{\mathcal{D}G^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}}\mathop{\mathcal{D}\bar{d}_{\mu}}\mathop{\mathcal{D}d_{\nu}}|\det\mathchoice{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}(h)|^{1/2}.

The generating functional (
153
) is equivalent to that of the second-order gravity (SOGR) formulation. However, the action appearing in Eq. (
153
) corresponds to the first-order formulation of gravity, namely the HP action. In addition, an extra quadratic term in the source
J_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}
arises.

This demonstrates the quantum equivalence between these formulations. The determinant factor in the measure (
154
) may be interpreted as the Senjanović determinant, which arises due to second-class constraints in the FOGR formalism. The second-class constraints, in turn, appear from the presence of additional fields, namely, the auxiliary field
G^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}
.

The manifestly non-covariant Senjanović determinant obtained in
[
8
]
, in our notation, reads

(155)

\Delta_{\text{FS}}^{1/2}(H)={}\det\left[h^{00}\left(\delta_{i}^{l}\delta_{k}^{j}-\frac{1}{D-1}\delta_{i}^{j}\delta_{k}^{l}\right)\right]D_{2}(H/h),

where

(156)

\begin{split}D_{2}(H/h)=\det\bigg\{\frac{1}{h^{00}}\bigg[\frac{1}{D-1}&\left(\delta^{j}_{i}\delta^{m}_{l}H^{kn}+\delta^{k}_{i}\delta^{m}_{l}H^{jn}+\delta^{j}_{i}\delta^{n}_{l}H^{km}+\delta^{k}_{i}\delta^{n}_{l}H^{jm}\right)\\
{}-{}&\left(\delta^{m}_{i}\delta^{j}_{l}H^{kn}+\delta^{m}_{i}\delta^{k}_{l}H^{jn}+\delta^{n}_{i}\delta^{j}_{l}H^{km}+\delta^{n}_{i}\delta^{k}_{l}H^{jm}\right)\bigg]\bigg\}\end{split}

with
H^{ij}\equiv h^{00}h^{ij}-h^{0i}h^{0j}
. Note that the terms
h^{00}
cancel, so that the Senjanović determinant only depends on
H^{ij}
. The first determinant in Eq. (
155
) can be discarded, leaving us with the determinant

(157)

D_{2}(H)=\det\mathchoice{M^{{{jk}{mn}}}_{{{i}\kern 5.30444pt{l}\kern 9.46999pt}}}{M^{{{jk}{mn}}}_{{{i}\kern 5.30444pt{l}\kern 9.46999pt}}}{M^{{{jk}{mn}}}_{{{i}\kern 4.43958pt{l}\kern 8.14249pt}}}{M^{{{jk}{mn}}}_{{{i}\kern 4.43958pt{l}\kern 8.14249pt}}}(H),

which resembles the determinant factor appearing in Eq. (
154
). This strongly indicates that the determinant in Eq. (
154
) is the manifestly covariant formulation of the Senjanović determinant in Eq. (
155
) found in Ref.
[
8
]
. Moreover, we have already shown that this is valid for the first-order formulation of YM theory, which is analogous to first-order gravity.

Introducing a flat background metric
h^{\mu\nu}=\eta^{\mu\nu}+\kappa\phi^{\mu\nu}
in Eq. (
153
) yields

(158)

\begin{split}Z_{\text{HP}}[j,J]={}&N\int\mathop{\mathcal{D}\phi^{\mu\nu}}\mathop{\mathcal{D}G^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}}\mathop{\mathcal{D}\bar{d}_{\mu}}\mathop{\mathcal{D}d_{\nu}}|\det\mathchoice{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}(h)|^{1/2}\\
&\qquad\times\exp{i\left[\int\mathop{d^{D}x}\left({\mathcal{L}}_{\text{eff}}^{\text{I}}+J_{\lambda}^{\mu\nu}G^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}+{j}_{\mu\nu}\phi^{\mu\nu}\right)\right]},\end{split}

where the effective Lagrangian is

(159)

{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{eff}}^{\text{I}}=\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}G_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}\mathchoice{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}(h)G_{\pi\tau}^{\rho}-\frac{1}{\kappa}G_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}\phi^{\mu\nu}_{,\lambda}-\frac{1}{2\alpha}(\partial_{\mu}\phi^{\mu\nu})^{2}+{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{gh}}(\phi).

Using the linearity of
\mathchoice{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}(h)
,

(160)

M(h)=M(\eta)+\kappa M(\phi),

in Eq. (
159
), we get

(161)

\begin{split}{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{eff}}^{\text{I}}&=\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}G_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}\mathchoice{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}(\eta)G_{\pi\tau}^{\rho}-\frac{1}{\kappa}G_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}\phi^{\mu\nu}_{,\lambda}-\frac{1}{2\alpha}(\partial_{\mu}\phi^{\mu\nu})^{2}+\bar{d}_{\mu}\partial^{2}\eta^{\mu\nu}d_{\nu}\\
&+\frac{1}{2\kappa}G_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}\mathchoice{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}(\phi)G_{\pi\tau}^{\rho}+\kappa\bar{d}_{\mu}\left[(\phi^{\rho\sigma}_{,\rho})\partial_{\sigma}\eta^{\mu\nu}-(\phi^{\rho\mu}_{,\rho})\partial^{\nu}+\phi^{\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\sigma}\eta^{\mu\nu}-(\partial_{\rho}\partial^{\nu}\phi^{\rho\mu})\right]d_{\nu}.\end{split}

From Eq. (
161
), we see that mixed propagators

\langle 0|T\phi^{\mu\nu}G_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda}|0\rangle_{\text{free}}
and

\langle 0|TG_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda}\phi^{\mu\nu}|0\rangle_{\text{free}}
arise.
We also have the momentum-independent vertex
(G\phi\phi)
.
The complete set of FOGR Feynman rules used in this work is presented in Appendix
10
.

We note that the propagator matrix can be diagonalized by a shift, leading to the
diagonal first-order gravity
(DFOGR) formulation
[
58
]
, which is reviewed briefly in Section
7.3
. This diagonalization facilitates the explicit verification of the structural identities derived for first-order gravity
[
10
]
, which is considered in Section
9
.

7.2
BRST symmetry

The BRST symmetry of the action in Eq. (
158
) reads

(162a)

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}\phi^{\mu\nu}=h^{\mu\rho}\partial_{\rho}d^{\nu}-h^{\nu\rho}\partial_{\rho}d^{\mu}-\partial_{\rho}(h^{\mu\nu}d^{\rho}),

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}{G}{{}_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}}=\kappa\bigg[-\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}d^{\lambda}+\frac{1}{2}(\delta_{\mu}^{\lambda}\partial_{\nu}+\delta_{\nu}^{\lambda}\partial_{\mu})\partial_{\rho}d^{\rho}-d^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}{G}{{}^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}}

(162b)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+{G}{{}^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}}\partial_{\rho}d^{\lambda}-({G}{{}^{\lambda}_{\mu\rho}}\partial_{\nu}+{G}{{}^{\lambda}_{\nu\rho}}\partial_{\mu})d^{\rho}\bigg],

(162c)

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}d^{\mu}=\kappa d^{\rho}d^{\mu}_{,\rho},

(162d)

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}\bar{d}^{\mu}=B^{\mu}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{s}B^{\mu}=0,

where
B^{\mu}
is the Nakanishi-Lautrup field.

In the FOGR, the extended source term is given by

(163)

\begin{split}&j^{\mu\nu}\phi_{\mu\nu}+{J}{{}_{\lambda}^{\mu\nu}}{G}{{}^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}}+i(\bar{\eta}_{\mu}d^{\mu}-\bar{d}^{\mu}\eta_{\mu})+j_{\mu}B^{\mu}\\
&+K_{\mu\nu}\left[h^{\mu\rho}\partial_{\rho}d^{\nu}-h^{\nu\rho}\partial_{\rho}d^{\mu}-\partial_{\rho}(h^{\mu\nu}d^{\rho})\right]+\kappa K_{\mu}d^{\rho}d^{\mu}_{,\rho}\\
&+\kappa{K}{{}_{\lambda}^{\mu\nu}}\bigg[-\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}d^{\lambda}+\frac{1}{2}(\delta_{\mu}^{\lambda}\partial_{\nu}+\delta_{\nu}^{\lambda}\partial_{\mu})\partial_{\rho}d^{\rho}-d^{\rho}{G}{{}^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}}+{G}{{}^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}}\partial_{\rho}d^{\lambda}-({G}{{}^{\lambda}_{\mu\rho}}\partial_{\nu}+{G}{{}^{\lambda}_{\nu\rho}}\partial_{\mu})d^{\rho}\bigg].\end{split}

The corresponding Zinn-Justin master equation is given by

(164)

\int\mathop{dx}\left(\frac{\delta_{R}\Gamma}{\delta\phi^{\mu\nu}}\frac{\delta_{L}\Gamma}{\delta K_{\mu\nu}}+\frac{\delta_{R}\Gamma}{\delta{G}{{}^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}}}\frac{\delta_{L}\Gamma}{\delta{K}{{}_{\lambda}^{\mu\nu}}}+\frac{\delta_{R}\Gamma}{\delta d^{\mu}}\frac{\delta_{L}\Gamma}{\delta K_{\mu}}+\frac{\delta_{R}\Gamma}{\delta\bar{d}^{\mu}}\frac{\delta_{L}\Gamma}{\delta j_{\mu}}\right)=0,

which is based on the invariance of the generating functional (
158
) under the FOGR BRST symmetry. The Slavnov-Taylor identities of the first-order formulation of gravity are derived by taking functional derivatives of this master equation.

7.3
Diagonal first-order formulation

In this section, we briefly examine the DFOGR formalism. In this formulation the mixed propagators are absent. However, we increase the number of interaction vertices. Even so, there is still a significant simplification in this formulation compared to the SOGR theory involves an infinite number of interaction vertices.

The diagonal formulation can be obtained with the field redefinition
[
11
]

(165)

G^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}={H}^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}+\mathchoice{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}(\eta)h^{\pi\tau}{}_{,\rho}

in Eq. (
140
), which leads to the following Lagrangian

(166)

\begin{split}{\mathcal{L}}^{\textrm{Id}}_{\textrm{HP}}={}&\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}{H}_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}\mathchoice{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}(\eta){H}_{\pi\tau}^{\rho}-\frac{1}{2}{\phi}_{,\lambda}^{\mu\nu}\mathchoice{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}(\eta){\phi}_{,\rho}^{\pi\tau}\\
&+\frac{1}{2\kappa}[{H}_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}+\kappa{\phi}_{,\rho}^{\alpha\beta}\mathchoice{(M^{-1})^{{{\rho}\kern 5.58418pt{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt}}_{{{\alpha\beta}{\mu\nu}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\rho}\kern 5.58418pt{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt}}_{{{\alpha\beta}{\mu\nu}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\rho}\kern 4.88953pt{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt}}_{{{\alpha\beta}{\mu\nu}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\rho}\kern 4.88953pt{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt}}_{{{\alpha\beta}{\mu\nu}}}}(\eta)]\mathchoice{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\sigma}\kern 3.73024pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\sigma}\kern 3.73024pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\sigma}\kern 3.48792pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\sigma}\kern 3.48792pt}}}(\phi)[{H}_{\pi\tau}^{\sigma}+\kappa\mathchoice{(M^{-1})^{{{\sigma}\kern 3.73024pt{\upsilon}\kern 3.40466pt}}_{{{\pi\tau}{\gamma\delta}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\sigma}\kern 3.73024pt{\upsilon}\kern 3.40466pt}}_{{{\pi\tau}{\gamma\delta}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\sigma}\kern 3.48792pt{\upsilon}\kern 3.047pt}}_{{{\pi\tau}{\gamma\delta}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\sigma}\kern 3.48792pt{\upsilon}\kern 3.047pt}}_{{{\pi\tau}{\gamma\delta}}}}(\eta){\phi}_{,\upsilon}^{\gamma\delta}].\end{split}

The field redefinition in Eq. (
165
) is simply a shift without any non-local terms. Therefore, the DFOGR is equivalent to the FOGR and SOGR formulation of gravity, even at the quantum level.

In order to quantize the theory, we can use the same measure in Eq. (
158
). Let us define the effective action of the DFOGR as

(167)

\mathcal{L}^{\textrm{Id}}_{\textrm{eff}}={\mathcal{L}}_{\textrm{HP}}^{\textrm{Id}}+{\mathcal{L}}_{\textrm{gf}}(\phi)+\mathcal{L}_{\textrm{gh}}(\phi).

Then, the generating functional is given by

(168)

\begin{split}Z_{\text{HP}}^{\text{Id}}[j,J]={}&N\int\mathop{\mathcal{D}\phi^{\mu\nu}}\mathop{\mathcal{D}H_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}}\mathop{\mathcal{D}\bar{d}_{\mu}}\mathop{\mathcal{D}d_{\nu}}|\det\mathchoice{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}(h)|^{1/2}\\
&\qquad\times\exp{i\int\mathop{d^{D}x}\left({\mathcal{L}}_{\text{eff}}^{\text{Id}}+J_{\lambda}^{\mu\nu}{H}_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}+{j}_{\mu\nu}\phi^{\mu\nu}\right)}.\end{split}

From Eq. (
166
), we observe that the auxiliary field and the metric are no longer coupled. Consequently, there are no mixed propagators. On the other hand, we have two additional vertices
(H\phi\phi)
and
(\phi\phi\phi)
that are momentum-dependent.

In the next chapter, we explore the consequences of the quantum equivalence in greater detail. We derive identities that relate Green’s function in the first-order formulation to those of composite fields computed in the second-order formulation. These structural identities can be regarded as identities of the first-order gravity itself, suggesting a novel physical interpretation of the auxiliary field.

Chapter 4
Quantum equivalence

In this chapter, we review the quantum equivalence between the first- and second-order formulations of YM and gravity theories, following the results of Ref.
[
11
]
. We also explore this quantum equivalence through the effective action,
\Gamma
, which generates the 1PI Green’s functions. Finally, we demonstrate that the contributions from the Senjanović determinant play a crucial role in verifying structural identities between the Green’s functions of the first- and second-order formulations of YM and gravity theories, as originally derived in Ref.
[
10
]
, at the integrand level.

8
Yang-Mills theory

By shifting the auxiliary field in the generating functional of the FOYM in Eq. (
93
)

(169)

\mathcal{F}^{a}_{\mu\nu}\to\mathring{\mathcal{F}}^{a}_{\mu\nu}+(\partial_{\mu}A^{a}_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}A^{a}_{\mu}+gf^{abc}A^{b}_{\mu}A^{c}_{\nu}),

which must leave it unchanged, we find

(170)

Z_{\text{1YM}}^{\prime}[0]=N\int\mathcal{D}\mathring{\mathcal{F}}_{\mu\nu}^{a}\exp i\int\mathop{dx}\left(\frac{1}{4}\mathring{\mathcal{F}}^{a}_{\mu\nu}\mathring{\mathcal{F}}^{a\,\mu\nu}\right)\int\mathop{\mathcal{D}A^{a}_{\mu}}\mathcal{D}c^{a}\mathcal{D}\bar{c}^{a}\exp i\int\mathop{dx}{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{eff}}^{(2)}.

We observe that the effective action turns out to be the SOYM effective action (
35
).
Absorbing the first integral in the normalization constant defining

(171)

N^{\prime}=N\int\mathcal{D}\mathring{\mathcal{F}}_{\mu\nu}^{a}\exp i\int\mathop{dx}\left(\frac{1}{4}\mathring{\mathcal{F}}^{a}_{\mu\nu}\mathring{\mathcal{F}}^{{a\,\mu}\nu}\right),

we get

(172)

Z^{\prime}_{\text{1YM}}[0]=Z_{\text{1YM}}[0]=Z_{\text{2YM}}[0].

This demonstrate the quantum equivalence between the first and second-order formulation of Yang-Mills theory, which is consistent with our discussion in Section
5.4
.

With the inclusion of sources, we generalize the shift (
169
), as shown in Ref.
[
9
]
, to

(173)

{\mathcal{F}}^{a}_{\mu\nu}\to\mathring{\mathcal{F}}^{a}_{\mu\nu}+{F}^{a}_{\mu\nu}-2J^{a}_{\mu\nu},

the generating functional (
93
) is then given by

(174)

Z^{\prime}_{\text{1YM}}[j,J]=N^{\prime}\int\mathop{\mathcal{D}A^{a}_{\mu}}\mathcal{D}c^{a}\mathcal{D}\bar{c}^{a}\exp i\int\mathop{dx}\big[{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{eff}}^{(2)}+j^{a\,\mu}{A}_{\mu}^{a}+{J}^{{a\,\mu\nu}}{F}_{\mu\nu}^{a}-J^{a\,\mu\nu}{J}_{\mu\nu}^{a}\big].

When
J=0
,

(175)

Z_{\text{1YM}}[j,0]=Z^{\prime}_{\text{1YM}}[j,0]=Z_{\text{2YM}}[j],

which establishes that the quantum equivalence holds when the sources are present.

Functional derivatives on both sides of Eq. (
175
) with respect to
j
generates Green’s function of the field
A
, respectively, in FOYM and SOYM theory:

(176)

\begin{split}\left.(-i)^{n}\frac{\delta^{n}Z_{\text{1YM}}[j,J]}{\delta j^{a_{1}}_{\mu_{1}}(x_{1})\cdots\delta{j}_{\mu_{n}}^{a_{n}}(x_{n})}\right|_{J=j=0}={}&\left.(-i)^{n}\frac{\delta^{n}Z_{\text{2YM}}[j]}{\delta j^{a_{1}}_{\mu_{1}}(x_{1})\cdots\delta{j}_{\mu_{n}}^{a_{n}}(x_{n})}\right|_{j=0}\\
\equiv{}&\langle 0|TA^{a_{1}\,\mu_{1}}(x_{1})\cdots A^{{a_{n}\,\mu_{n}}}(x_{n})|0\rangle.\end{split}

Therefore, the
n
-point Green’s functions of the gauge field
A
are the same in both formulations. This result has been explicitly verified for
n=4
at the tree-level
[
11
]
,
n=2
(up to the one-loop order) in Ref.
[
5
]
(by using the background field method in
[
12
]
) and at finite temperature
[
72
]
.

8.1
Structural identities

Now, for
J\neq 0
,

(177)

Z_{\text{1YM}}[j,J]=Z^{\prime}_{\text{1YM}}[j,J]\equiv Z_{\text{2YM}}[j,J].

We can obtain structural identities
[
9
]
that relate Green’s functions of the auxiliary field
F
with Green’s function in the SOYM formalism of the field strength tensor
{F}
. As we have shown, the field strength tensor
{F}_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)=\partial_{\mu}A^{a}_{\nu}(x)-\partial_{\nu}A^{a}_{\mu}(x)+gf^{abc}A^{b}_{\mu}(x)A^{c}_{\nu}(x)

is a composite operator, which is equal to the auxiliary field
\mathcal{F}_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)
at the classical level. These structural identities are the realization of this equality at the quantum level.

Some of these structural identities are (see
[
9
,
11
]
for a detailed derivation)

(178a)

\displaystyle\langle 0|T\mathcal{F}^{a\,\mu\nu}(x)|0\rangle={}

\displaystyle\langle 0|T{F}^{a\,\mu\nu}(x)|0\rangle,

(178b)

\displaystyle\langle 0|T\mathcal{F}^{a\,\mu\nu}(x)A^{b\,\rho}(y)|0\rangle={}

\displaystyle\langle 0|T{F}^{a\,\mu\nu}(x)A^{b\,\rho}(y)|0\rangle,

(178c)

\displaystyle\langle 0|T\mathcal{F}^{a\,\mu\nu}(x)\mathcal{F}^{b\,\rho\sigma}(y)|0\rangle={}

\displaystyle\langle 0|T{F}^{a\,\mu\nu}(x){F}^{b\,\rho\sigma}(y)|0\rangle+2i\delta^{ab}I^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\delta(x-y).

In principle, the left-hand side of Eq. (
178
) is computed in the FOYM formalism while the right-hand side is in the SOYM. However, from Eq. (
176
), we see that any Green’s function in the SOYM can also be computed equivalently in the FOYM. Consequently, we can also state that these structural identities are identities between FOYM Green’s functions. For instance, we have that

(179)

\left\langle 0|T(\mathcal{F}-{F})^{a\,\mu\nu}(x)|0\right\rangle=0

in which Green’s function of the composite operator is also computed in the FOYM formalism. Therefore, the composite field Green’s functions can be easily evaluated in the FOYM formalism. It also relates the renormalization of the auxiliary field
\mathcal{F}
with the renormalization of the composite field.

We can easily generalize the structural identities Eq. (
178
), or even obtain more involved ones as

(180)

\begin{split}\langle 0|T\mathcal{F}^{a\,\mu\nu}(x)\mathcal{F}^{b\,\rho\sigma}(y)\mathcal{F}^{c\,\alpha\beta}(z)A^{d\,\gamma}(w)|0\rangle={}&\langle 0|T{F}^{a\,\mu\nu}(x){F}^{b\,\rho\sigma}(y){F}^{c\,\alpha\beta}(z)A^{d\,\gamma}(w)|0\rangle\\
&+2i\delta^{ab}I^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\delta(x-y)\langle 0|T{F}^{c\,\alpha\beta}(z)A^{d\,\gamma}(w)|0\rangle\\
&+2i\delta^{bc}I^{\rho\sigma\alpha\beta}\delta(y-z)\langle 0|T{F}^{a\,\mu\nu}(x)A^{d\,\gamma}(w)|0\rangle\\
&+2i\delta^{ac}I^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\delta(x-z)\langle 0|T{F}^{b\,\rho\sigma}(y)A^{d\,\gamma}(w)|0\rangle.\end{split}

Note the presence of the contact term in Eq. (
178c
) revealing the quantum nature of the auxiliary
F
. These terms also appear in the Ward-Takahashi identities
[
73
]
and in the Dyson-Schwinger equations
[
74
,
75
]
.

Although these structural identities are not the usual Slanov-Taylor identities (derived in Chapter
2
) they can be obtained alternatively by a similar procedure using the symmetry
[
7
]

(181)

\delta\mathcal{F}^{a}_{\mu\nu}=f^{abc}(\mathcal{F}^{b}_{\mu\nu}-{F}^{b}_{\mu\nu})\zeta^{c},\quad\delta A^{a}_{\mu}=0

of the FOYM.
8
8

8

We thank Prof. Josif Frenkel for this clarification.
We can recast Eq. (
181
) as

(182)

\delta\mathcal{F}_{\mu\nu}^{a}=2\eta^{ab}\frac{\delta S_{\text{1YM}}}{\delta\mathcal{F}^{b\,\mu\nu}},

where
\eta^{ab}=f^{abc}\zeta^{c}=-\eta^{ba}
.
Symmetries of this form are referred to as
trivial symmetries

[
55
]
, since the variation of the action vanishes trivially:

(183)

\delta{S_{\text{1YM}}}=\frac{\delta S_{\text{1YM}}}{\delta\mathcal{F}^{b\,\mu\nu}}\delta\mathcal{F}^{b\,\mu\nu}=2\eta^{bc}\frac{\delta S_{\text{1YM}}}{\delta\mathcal{F}^{b\,\mu\nu}}\frac{\delta S_{\text{1YM}}}{\delta\mathcal{F}^{c\,\mu\nu}}=0,

a condition that is independent of structure of the action.

The invariance of Eq. (
93
) under the symmetry in Eq. (
181
) means that

(184)

\begin{split}\delta Z_{\text{1YM}}[j,J;K]={}&\int\mathop{\mathcal{D}\mathcal{F}_{\mu\nu}^{a}}\mathop{\mathcal{D}A_{\mu}^{a}}J^{a}_{\mu\nu}\delta\mathcal{F}^{a\,\mu\nu}\exp{i(S_{\text{1YM}}+S_{\text{src}}[j,J;K]})\\
={}&\int\mathop{dx}f^{abc}J_{\mu\nu}^{a}\left(\frac{\delta Z_{1YM}}{\delta J_{\mu\nu}^{b}}-\frac{\delta Z_{\text{1YM}}}{\delta K_{\mu\nu}^{b}}\right)\zeta^{c}=0,\end{split}

where
S_{\text{1YM}}
is the FOYM action. We extended the source term to

(185)

S_{\text{src}}[j,J;K]=\int\mathop{dx}(j_{\mu}^{a}A^{a\,\mu}+J_{\mu\nu}^{a}\mathcal{F}^{a\,\mu\nu}+K_{\mu\nu}^{a}F^{a\,\mu\nu}),

introducing the source
K_{\mu\nu}^{a}
for
F^{a\,\mu\nu}
.

Taking functional derivatives of Eq. (
184
) with respect to the sources
j
and
J
leads to structural identities, such as Eqs. (
178a
) and (
178b
). However, the fundamental identity (
176
) does not follow from Eq. (
184
). Hence, these Slanov-Taylor-like identities arising from the trivial symmetry in Eq. (
181
) are insufficient to ensure the quantum equivalence.

8.2
Structural identities at one-loop order

Here, we briefly review the verification of the structural identities in Eq. (
178
). This is done in great detail in
[
11
]
, and in the appendix of Ref.
[
9
]
.

The explicit verification of the structural identities at the tree-level is straightforward. For instance, the left-hand side of Eq. (
178b
) reads

(186)

\langle 0|TF^{a\,\mu\nu}A^{b\,\rho}|0\rangle=\partial^{\mu}\langle 0|TA^{a\,\nu}A^{b\,\rho}|0\rangle-\partial^{\nu}\langle 0|TA^{a\,\mu}A^{b\,\rho}|0\rangle.

By using that
\partial^{\mu}=ip^{\mu}
, and the standard form of the
\langle 0|TA^{a\mu}(-p)A^{b\nu}(p)|0\rangle_{\text{free}}
, which is given by

(187)

-\frac{i}{p^{2}}\left(\eta^{\mu\nu}-(1-\alpha)\frac{p^{\mu}p^{\nu}}{p^{2}}\right)\delta^{ab}

in the Lorenz gauge, we find that

(188)

\langle 0|TF^{a\,\mu\nu}A^{b\,\rho}|0\rangle=\frac{p^{\mu}\eta^{\nu\rho}-p^{\nu}\eta^{\mu\rho}}{p^{2}}\delta^{ab}=\langle 0|T\mathcal{F}^{a\,\mu\nu}A^{b\,\rho}|0\rangle

(cf. Eq. (
423c
)).

On the other hand, at one-loop order we have to compute
pinched diagrams
(see Fig.
1
). Composite fields, such as the curvature
F_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)
, in which two or more fields are taken in the same space-time point, lead to ultraviolet singularities.

Figure 1:
Pinched diagrams. Diagrams (a) and (b) respectively represent the contributions to the left-hand side of the structural identities (
178a
) and (
178b
) at one-loop order.

The standard procedure to compute these diagrams is using a limit process, for instance, the diagram in Fig.
1
(a) is given by “pinching” their external legs:

(189)

\displaystyle\lim_{z\to x}\langle 0|TA(x)A(z)A(y)|0\rangle=\langle 0|TA(x)^{2}A(z)|0\rangle,

where
A(x)^{2}
is a composite field.
In
[
11
]
, the Feynman rules were extended by introducing the vertex
(\mathbb{H}AA)
shown in Fig.
2
, where

(190)

\mathbb{H}_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)=gf^{abc}A_{\mu}^{b}(x)A_{\nu}^{c}(x)

is the composite part of
F_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)
.

Figure 2:
Extended Feynman rule for the interaction vertex with a composite field
\mathbb{H}_{\mu\nu}^{a}
, which is represented by a double spring line.

This vertex allows us to construct diagrams that contribute to Green’s functions of the composite field
\mathbb{H}_{\mu\nu}^{a}
. For instance, the pinched diagrams in Fig.
1
are represented by the standard diagrams in Fig.
3
using the extended Feynman rule in Fig.
2
. The computation of the pinched diagrams can be done using standard methods (this also includes the symmetry factor).

Figure 3:
Pinched diagrams of Fig.
1
represented by extended Feynman rules. The symmetry factors are the same of those of the original pinched diagrams.

Contributions to the right-hand side of the structural identities (
178
) are given by pinched diagrams, while the left-hand side is the usual Green’s function computed in the FOYM.
The extended Feynman rule
(hAA)
can be written as
[
11
]

(191)

(\mathbb{H}_{\mu\nu}^{a}A_{\rho}^{b}A_{\sigma}^{c})\equiv\langle 0|TH_{\mu\nu}^{a}H_{\alpha\beta}^{d}|0\rangle_{\text{free}}(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha\beta}^{d}A_{\rho}^{b}A_{\sigma}^{c})=2i(\mathcal{F}_{\mu\nu}^{a}A_{\rho}^{b}A_{\sigma}^{c}),

where
(\mathcal{F}_{\mu\nu}^{a}A_{\rho}^{b}A_{\sigma}^{c})
is the
(FAA)
vertex defined in Eq. (
426
) and
\langle 0|TH_{\mu\nu}^{a}H_{\alpha\beta}^{d}|0\rangle_{\text{free}}=2iI_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}
is the free propagator of the auxiliary field
H_{\mu\nu}^{a}
obtained by the diagonalization of the propagator matrix of the FOYM theory
[
58
]
.
Using this rule, one can derive equivalent diagrams that contribute to Green’s functions of the FOYM
[
11
]
.

For instance, it is shown in Fig.
4
the equivalence of the pinched diagrams in Fig.
3
to the contributions of the Green’s functions of the diagonal FOYM theory: (a) the mixed propagator
\langle 0|TH_{\mu\nu}^{a}A_{\rho}^{b}|0\rangle
and (b) the propagator of the auxiliary field
\langle 0|TH_{\mu\nu}^{a}H_{\rho\sigma}^{b}|0\rangle
.
In the non-diagonal FOYM theory, the pinched contributions of the left-hand side of Eq. (
178
) combine these diagrams.

Figure 4:
Green’s functions (at one-loop order) of the FOYM formalism equivalent to the pinched diagrams in Fig.
3
.

Hence, the structural identities hold at the integrand level. No explicit diagram computations are needed for their verification. This is interesting since they arise directly from the quantum equivalence between the first- and second-order formulations of the YM theory (and gravity). The generality of the quantum equivalence itself is reflected in the generality of the structural identities.

Moreover, verifying these identities at the integrand level confirms their validity at any dimensions and gauge. Furthermore, they are independent of the regularization scheme
[
11
]
.
In Section
9
, we show that this is also valid, under certain conditions, in the context of gravity. This shall extend even further the work in
[
11
]
, illuminating the quantum equivalence of the first- and second-order formulation of gravity.

8.3
Effective action

The quantum equivalence derived using the generating functional
Z[j]
can also be appreciated through other functionals, such as the connected generating functional
W[j]
and the effective action
\Gamma[{\phi}_{c}]
.

By the definition of the connected generating functional

(192)

W[j]=-i\ln Z[j]

and from Eq. (
177
), we get

(193)

W_{\text{1YM}}[j,J]=W_{\text{1YM}}^{\prime}[j,J]=W_{\text{2YM}}[j,J].

A functional derivative in both sides of Eq. (
193
) with respect to
j
leads to the connected Green’s function
\left\langle\phi\right\rangle_{\mathsf{C}}
. Meanwhile, a functional derivative in the left-hand side of Eq. (
193
) with respect to
J
gives
\left\langle\mathcal{F}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{C}}
and, in the right-hand side, yields the connected Green’s function of the composite field
F
, which is equal to the classical value of
\mathcal{F}
. That is, we obtain the same structural identities derived using
Z
, however, now only connected diagrams contribute:

(194)

\left\langle\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{n}\mathcal{F}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{F}_{m}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{C}}=\left\langle\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{n}{F}_{1}\cdots{F}_{m}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{C}}+\text{contact terms,}

the subscript
\mathsf{C}
denotes connected Green’s functions.

Let us introduce the generating functional

(195)

Z^{(2)}_{\text{YM}}[j,J]=N\int\mathcal{D}A^{a}_{\mu}\mathcal{D}c^{a}\mathcal{D}\bar{c}^{a}\exp i\int\mathop{dx}\left({\mathcal{L}}_{\text{eff}}^{(2)}+j^{a\,\mu}{A}_{\mu}^{a}+{J}^{{a\,\mu\nu}}{F}_{\mu\nu}^{a}\right).

We have that

(196)

Z^{(2)}_{\text{YM}}=Z_{\text{1YM}}^{\prime}[j,J]\exp\left(i\int\mathop{dx}J_{\mu\nu}^{a}J^{a\,\mu\nu}\right),

taking the logarithm of the expression above leads to

(197)

W^{(2)}_{\text{YM}}[j,J]=W_{\text{1YM}}[j,J]+\int\mathop{dx}J_{\mu\nu}^{a}J^{a\,\mu\nu},

where we have used Eq. (
193
).
The left-hand side of this relation leads to Green’s functions involving the field
A
and the composite field
F
in the SOYM, while the right-side leads to Green’s functions with external fields
A
and the auxiliary field
\mathcal{F}
. The unusual quadratic dependence on the source is now factored, and Green’s functions are derived in a standard way.

Thus, Eq. (
197
) can be used to derive structural identities straightforwardly.
For example, the functional derivative
\delta^{2}/\delta J_{\alpha\beta}^{a}\delta J_{\mu\nu}^{b}
applied in Eq. (
197
) leads directly to a particular case of Eq. (
194
) analogous to Eq. (
178c
) (see Eq. (
211
)).

Now we can discuss the quantum equivalence using effective actions.
The effective action
\Gamma[\phi_{\mathsf{c}}]
can be constructed using a Legendre transform of the functional
W[J]
. The conjugated variable to the source
J
is the classical field
\phi_{\mathsf{c}}
, the functional derivative of
W[J]
with respect to
J
.
Hence, let us define the classical fields

(198a)

\displaystyle\frac{\delta W_{\text{YM}}^{(2)}[j,J]}{\delta j_{\mu}^{a}}={}

\displaystyle A^{a\,\mu}_{\mathsf{c}},

(198b)

\displaystyle\frac{\delta W_{\text{YM}}^{(2)}[j,J]}{\delta J_{\mu\nu}^{a}}={}

\displaystyle F_{\mathsf{c}}^{a\,\mu\nu}+\mathfrak{F}^{a\,\mu\nu},

(198c)

\displaystyle\frac{\delta W_{\text{1YM}}{[j,J]}}{\delta j_{\mu}^{a}}={}

\displaystyle A^{a\,\mu}_{\mathsf{c}},

(198d)

\displaystyle\frac{\delta W_{\text{1YM}}{[j,J]}}{\delta J_{\mu\nu}^{a}}={}

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{a\,\mu\nu}_{\mathsf{c}},

(198e)

\displaystyle\frac{\delta W_{\text{2YM}}[j]}{\delta j_{\mu}^{a}}={}

\displaystyle A^{a\,\mu}_{\mathsf{c}},

where
F_{\mathsf{c}}^{a\,\mu\nu}\equiv F^{a\,\mu\nu}(A_{\mathsf{c}})
. The subscript
\mathsf{c}
will be omitted from now on.

This allows us to construct the following effective actions:

(199)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\text{1YM}}[A,\mathcal{F}]={}

\displaystyle W_{\text{1YM}}[j,J]-j_{\mu}^{a}{A}^{a\,\mu}-J_{\mu\nu}^{a}\mathcal{F}^{a\,\mu\nu},

(200)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(2)}_{\text{YM}}[A,\mathfrak{F}]={}

\displaystyle W_{\text{YM}}^{(2)}[j,J]-j_{\mu}^{a}A^{a\,\mu}-J_{\mu\nu}^{a}F^{a\,\mu\nu}-J_{\mu\nu}^{a}\mathfrak{F}^{a\,\mu\nu},

(201)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\text{2YM}}[A]={}

\displaystyle W_{\text{2YM}}[j]-j_{\mu}^{a}A^{a\,\mu}.

The effective action
\Gamma^{(2)}_{\text{YM}}[A,\mathfrak{F}]
is the effective action of the composite field
F^{a\,\mu\nu}
, that is, the curvature.

The quantum equations of motion are

(202a)

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\Gamma^{(2)}_{\text{YM}}}{\delta A^{a\,\mu}(x)}={}

\displaystyle-j_{\mu}^{a}(x)-2D^{ab\,\nu}J^{b}_{\nu\mu}(x),

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\Gamma^{(2)}_{\text{YM }}}{\delta\mathfrak{F}^{a\,\mu\nu}(x)}=-J_{\mu\nu}^{a};

(202b)

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\Gamma_{\text{1YM}}}{\delta A^{a\,\mu}(x)}={}

\displaystyle-j_{\mu}^{a}(x),

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\Gamma_{\text{1YM }}}{\delta\mathcal{F}^{a\,\mu\nu}(x)}=-J_{\mu\nu}^{a}.

Differentiating Eq. (
197
) with respect to
J^{a\,\mu\nu}
and recalling the definition of the classical fields in Eq. (
198
), we obtain the fundamental identity

(203)

F_{\mu\nu}^{a}+\mathfrak{F}_{\mu\nu}^{a}=\mathcal{F}_{\mu\nu}^{a}+2J_{\mu\nu}^{a},

which is consistent with Eq. (
173
) when
\mathfrak{F}=0
.

From Eqs. (
199
) and (
200
), we find that

(204)

\Gamma_{\text{1YM}}[A,\mathcal{F}]=\Gamma^{(2)}_{\text{YM}}[A,\mathfrak{F}]+J_{\mu\nu}^{a}F^{a\,\mu\nu}+J_{\mu\nu}^{a}\mathfrak{F}^{a\,\mu\nu}-J_{\mu\nu}^{a}J^{a\,\mu\nu}-J_{\mu\nu}^{a}\mathcal{F}^{a\,\mu\nu}.

Finally, by replacing Eq. (
203
) in Eq. (
204
), we obtain that

(205)

\Gamma_{\text{1YM}}[A,\mathcal{F}]=\Gamma_{\text{YM}}^{(2)}[A,\mathcal{F}-F+2J]+J_{\mu\nu}^{a}J^{a\,\mu\nu},

or alternatively,

(206)

\Gamma_{\text{1YM}}[A,\mathcal{F}]=\Gamma_{\text{YM}}^{(2)}\left[A,\mathfrak{F}\right]+\frac{1}{4}(\mathfrak{F}_{\mu\nu}^{a}+F_{\mu\nu}^{a}-\mathcal{F}_{\mu\nu}^{a})^{2}.

This shows the quantum equivalence between the FOYM and SOYM analogously to Eqs. (
177
) and (
193
).
Setting
j=J=0
, we have that
[
76
]

(207)

\Gamma_{\text{1YM}}\left[A,\bar{F}(A)\right]=\Gamma^{(2)}_{\text{YM}}[A,\bar{F}(A)-F]=\Gamma_{\text{2YM}}[A],

where
\bar{F}(A)
is the value of
\mathcal{F}
in which

(208)

\frac{\delta\Gamma_{\text{1YM}}[A,\mathcal{F}]}{\delta\mathcal{F}}=0.

Note that, Eq. (
207
) is the analog of Eq. (
175
). It is the quantum realization of the classical equivalence between the action of the SOYM and the FOYM.

In order to obtain structural identities, we can use the relation

(209)

\Gamma^{(2)}_{\text{YM}}[A,J]=\Gamma_{\text{1YM}}[A,\mathcal{F}]+J_{\mu\nu}^{a}\mathcal{F}^{a\,\mu\nu}+J_{\mu\nu}^{a}J^{a\,\mu\nu}.

The effective action

(210)

\Gamma^{(2)}_{\text{YM}}[A,J]=W_{\text{YM}}^{(2)}[j,J]-j_{\mu}^{a}A^{a\,\mu}

is the standard effective action of the SOYM in which we introduced a source
J
to the composite field
F
.
The Legendre transform of the effective action
\Gamma^{(2)}_{\text{YM}}[A,J]
with respect to
J
(with the conjugate variables (
198
)) results in
\Gamma^{(2)}_{\text{YM}}[A,\mathfrak{F}]
.
For instance, taking
\delta^{2}/\delta J_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)\delta J_{\alpha\beta}^{b}(y)
and setting
j=J=0
on both sides of Eq. (
209
) yields

(211)

-\langle 0|TF^{a\,\mu\nu}(x)F^{b\,\mu\nu}(y)|0\rangle_{\mathsf{C}}=2I^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\delta(x-y)-\langle 0|T\mathcal{F}^{a\,\mu\nu}(x)\mathcal{F}^{b\,\mu\nu}(y)|0\rangle_{\mathsf{C}}.

Recall that these fields are not the classical fields defined in Eq. (
198
). The relations are given by
\langle 0|TF^{a\,\mu\nu}|0\rangle_{\mathsf{C},\,J}=F_{\mathsf{c}}^{a\,\mu\nu}+\mathfrak{F}^{a\,\mu\nu}-2J^{a\,\mu\nu}
and
\langle 0|T\mathcal{F}^{a\,\mu\nu}|0\rangle_{\mathsf{C},\,J}=\mathcal{F}^{a\,\mu\nu}_{\mathsf{c}}
.

The equivalence between the FOYM and SOYM effective actions indicates that the auxiliary field
\mathcal{F}
contributes to the 1PI Green’s functions of the gauge field
A
. We can understand the physical meaning of Eq. (
207
) by solving Eq. (
208
). At the lowest order in the loop expansion,
\Gamma_{\text{1YM}}[A,\mathcal{F}]=S_{\text{1YM}}
. Therefore, the solution to Eq. (
208
) is given by the classical solution of the equation of motion (
45
), leading to

(212)

\bar{F}_{\mu\nu}^{a}(A)=F_{\mu\nu}^{a}.

Indeed, we already know that substituting the auxiliary field
\mathcal{F}
with the curvature
F
in the FOYM action yields the SOYM action. Next, we will use Eq. (
212
) to compute the effective action
\Gamma_{\text{1YM}}[A,F]
at one-loop order. This result can be compared with the effective action of the SOYM.

8.4
Self-Energy

At one-loop order, the effective action
\Gamma_{\text{1YM}}[A,\bar{F}=F]
is given by

(213)

\int\mathop{dx}\left(\frac{1}{2}A\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.45}]{fig/SEAA-eps-converted-to.pdf}}}A+A\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.45}]{fig/SEAF-eps-converted-to.pdf}}}F+F\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.45}]{fig/SEFA-eps-converted-to.pdf}}}A+\frac{1}{2}F\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.45}]{fig/SEFF-eps-converted-to.pdf}}}F+\cdots\right),

where
\Sigma
denotes the one-loop self-energy. By the quantum equivalence (
207
), this is equal to the effective action of the SOYM
\Gamma_{\text{2YM}}[A]
, which is given by

(214)

\int\mathop{dx}\left(\frac{1}{2}A\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.45}]{fig/SEAA2YM-eps-converted-to.pdf}}}A+\cdots\right)

at one-loop order.
The dots denote terms of higher powers in the fields
A
and
F
. These terms are not relevant to the computation of the self-energy. Recall that the
self-energy
is obtained by taking the second-order functional derivative of the effective action and setting the quantum equations of motion. Thus, the resulting contribution coming from terms of order higher than two vanish, since the quantum equations of motion lead to
\langle 0|TA|0\rangle=\langle 0|TF|0\rangle=\langle 0|T\mathcal{F}|0\rangle=0
(sources vanishing).

Taking the functional derivative
\delta^{2}/\delta A_{\mu}^{a}\delta A_{\nu}^{b}
of Eq. (
213
) (and setting the quantum equations of motion) yields

(215)

\Sigma_{\mu\nu}^{ab}+\Sigma_{\mu}^{ac\,\alpha\beta}Q_{\alpha\beta\,\nu}^{cb}+Q_{\mu\,\alpha\beta}^{ac}\Sigma^{bc\,\alpha\beta}{}_{\nu}+Q^{ac}_{\mu\,\alpha\beta}\Sigma^{cd\,\alpha\beta\rho\sigma}Q^{db}_{\rho\sigma\,\nu}\,

where we defined

(216)

Q_{\nu\,\alpha\beta}^{cb}\equiv-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\delta F_{\alpha\beta}^{c}}{\delta A^{b\,\nu}}=-\partial^{\sigma}I_{\sigma\nu\,\alpha\beta}\delta^{cb}\quad\text{and}\quad Q_{\alpha\beta\,\nu}^{cb}=-Q_{\nu\,\alpha\beta}^{cb}.

We also defined the identity
I
in Eq. (
78
) and
\bm{\Sigma}_{\bullet\bullet}
are the components of the matrix:

(217)

\begin{pmatrix}\bm{\Sigma}_{AA}&\bm{\Sigma}_{A\mathcal{F}}\\
\bm{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{F}A}&\bm{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{F}}\end{pmatrix}_{\text{components}}=\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma_{\mu\nu}^{ab}&\Sigma_{\nu\,\alpha\beta}^{cb}\\
\Sigma_{\rho\sigma\,\mu}^{ad}&\Sigma^{cd}_{\alpha\beta\rho\sigma}\end{pmatrix}.

Computing Eq. (
215
) leads to the SOYM self-energy
\Sigma^{\prime}
, which verifies Eq. (
205
) at one-loop order.

On the other hand, it is well-known that the self-energy of the SOYM
\Sigma^{\prime}
differs from the
AA
self-energy in the FOYM, denoted by
\Sigma_{AA}

[
58
]
. The discussion in this section clarifies it, the self-energy of the SOYM can be obtained from the FOYM. Let us show another approach, we shall show that the effective
AA
self-energy
[
77
]
is given by Eq. (
215
). This is not a coincidence, the self-energy is related to the pole of the propagator with is associated with the particle’s mass. The naive self-energy
\Sigma_{AA}
is not the field
A
propagator pole, it is the effective self-energy.

We can compute the effective self-energies using the relation between the full propagator
G
, the (free) propagator
G_{0}
, and the self-energy
\Sigma
,
G^{-1}=G_{0}^{-1}+\Sigma

[
48
]
.
In the FOYM formulation, we have that

(218)

\mathbf{G}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{A}&\mathbf{Q}_{L}\\
\mathbf{Q}_{R}&\mathbf{D}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\bm{\Sigma}_{AA}&\bm{\Sigma}_{A\mathcal{F}}\\
\bm{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{F}A}&\bm{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{F}}\end{pmatrix},

where the components of the last matrix
\mathbf{\Sigma}
are the
naive self-energies
, which are obtained summing all 1PI diagrams.
The first matrix in the above expression is the Hessian of the FOYM action

(219)

\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{A}&\mathbf{Q}_{L}\\
\mathbf{Q}_{R}&\mathbf{D}\end{pmatrix}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\dfrac{\delta^{2}S_{\text{1YM}}}{\delta A_{\mu}^{a}\delta A_{\nu}^{b}}&\dfrac{\delta^{2}S_{\text{1YM}}}{\delta A_{\nu}^{b}\delta\mathcal{F}_{\alpha\beta}^{c}}\\
\dfrac{\delta^{2}S_{\text{1YM}}}{\delta\mathcal{F}_{\rho\sigma}^{d}\delta A_{\mu}^{a}}&\dfrac{\delta^{2}S_{\text{1YM}}}{\delta\mathcal{F}_{\rho\sigma}^{d}\delta\mathcal{F}_{\alpha\beta}^{c}}\end{pmatrix}.

The bold symbols are tensors in which the indices have been omitted for clarity.

The effective one-loop self-energy is defined by

(220)

\bm{\Sigma}_{\text{eff}}=(\mathbf{G_{0}})^{-1}-\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{0}^{-1}}\left[\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{A}&\mathbf{Q}_{L}\\
\mathbf{Q}_{R}&\mathbf{D}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\bm{\Sigma}_{AA}&\bm{\Sigma}_{A\mathcal{F}}\\
\bm{\Sigma}_{A\mathcal{F}}&\bm{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{F}}\end{pmatrix}\right]^{-1}\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{0}^{-1}}-\mathcal{O}(\bm{\Sigma}^{2}),

where
\mathcal{O}(\bm{\Sigma}^{2})
denotes all contributions of order greater than
\bm{\Sigma}
(one-loop).
The
effective self-energy
can be defined, at any order, as the sum of all 1PI diagrams that contributes to the amputated propagator
\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{0}^{1-}{G}\mathcal{G}_{0}^{-1}}
. Note that, there is a very subtle difference between the free propagator terms that appear in Eq. (
220
). We have
\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{0}^{-1}}
that denotes the matrix whose components are the inverse of the free propagators, while
(\mathbf{G_{0}})^{-1}
is the inverse of the matrix of free propagators (the components are given in Eq. (
219
)).

This should clarify the difference between the naive self-energy defined by Eq. (
218
), and the
proper self-energy
9
9

9

It can also be interpreted as an effective self-energy
[
77
]
.
defined by

(221)

\bm{\Sigma}_{\text{eff}}\equiv\mathbf{G^{-1}}-\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{0}^{-1}},

which is the appropriate definition for the self-energy in theories such as the FOYM in which the fields are mixed (the Hessian is not diagonal). Indeed, the proper self-energy can also be obtained directly from the effective action. The two-point 1PI Green’s functions are

(222)

\frac{\delta^{2}\Gamma_{\text{1YM}}}{\delta{}\bm{\Phi}\delta{}\bm{\Phi}}=-\frac{\delta{}\mathbf{J}}{\delta{}\bm{\Phi}},\quad{}\bm{\Phi}=\begin{pmatrix}A_{\mathsf{c}}\\
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{c}}\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad{}\mathbf{J}=\begin{pmatrix}j\\
J\end{pmatrix}.

One can see that by the definition of the classical fields, we have that

(223)

-\frac{\delta{}\mathbf{J}}{\delta{}\bm{\Phi}}=-\left(\frac{\delta^{2}W_{\text{1YM}}}{\delta{}\mathbf{J}\delta{}\mathbf{J}}\right)^{-1}=\mathbf{G}^{-1}.

This allows us rewrite the relation Eq. (
222
) and the definition of the self-energy (see
[
48
]
) as

(224)

\bm{\Sigma}=\frac{\delta^{2}\Gamma_{\text{1YM}}}{\delta{}\bm{\Phi}\delta{}\bm{\Phi}}-\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{0}^{-1}}=\mathbf{G}^{-1}-\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{0}^{-1}},

which is the same definition of the effective or proper self-energy in Eq. (
221
). Hence, the components of the naive self-energy in Eq. (
218
) can be seen as “partial self-energies”. Now, we show that the proper
AA
self-energy
({}\bm{\Sigma}_{\text{eff}})_{AA}
is obtained from these partial self-energies.

We can proceed by obtaining the full propagator by the inverse of the block matrix

(225)

\mathbf{G}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{A}+\bm{\Sigma}_{AA}&\mathbf{Q}_{L}+\bm{\Sigma}_{A\mathcal{F}}\\
\mathbf{Q}_{R}+\bm{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{F}A}&\mathbf{D}+\bm{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{F}}\end{pmatrix}^{-1}.

For this we can use the blockwise matrix inversion, for example,

(226)

\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{A}&\mathbf{Q}_{L}\\
\mathbf{Q}_{R}&\mathbf{D}\end{pmatrix}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{X}^{-1}&-\mathbf{X}^{-1}\mathbf{Q}_{L}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\\
-\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{Q}_{R}\mathbf{X}^{-1}&\mathbf{D}^{-1}+\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{Q}_{R}\mathbf{X}^{-1}\mathbf{Q}_{L}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\end{pmatrix},

where
\mathbf{X}
is the Schur complement:

(227)

\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{Q}_{L}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{Q}_{R}.

Since we are only interested in finding the proper (effective) self-energy of the gauge field
A
at one-loop order, the computation of the inverse of
\mathbf{G}
can be skipped.

In Eq. (
226
) we have the matrix of the propagators of the FOYM
\mathbf{G_{0}}
, which is the inverse of the Hessian. From Eq. (
220
), we have that

(228)

[\bm{\Sigma}_{\text{eff}}]_{AA}=\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{X}\bar{\mathbf{X}}^{-1}\mathbf{X}\Rightarrow\bar{\mathbf{X}}=\left(\mathbf{I}-[\bm{\Sigma}_{\text{eff}}]_{AA}\mathbf{X}^{-1}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{X}.

Restricting the above relation to one-loop order leads to

(229)

\bar{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{X}+[\bm{\Sigma}_{\text{eff}}]_{AA}.

Computing the Schur complement
\bar{\mathbf{X}}
of Eq. (
225
) (similar to Eq. (
227
)) yields

(230)

\bar{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{A}+\bm{\Sigma}_{AA}-(\mathbf{Q}_{L}+\bm{\Sigma}_{A\mathcal{F}})(\mathbf{D}+\bm{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{F}})^{-1}(\mathbf{Q}_{R}+\bm{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{F}A}).

Using that
(\mathbf{D}+\bm{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{F}})^{-1}=\mathbf{D}-\bm{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{F}}+\mathcal{O}(\bm{\Sigma}^{2}_{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{F}})
, we have

(231)

\bar{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{X}+\bm{\Sigma}_{AA}+\mathbf{Q}_{L}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{F}}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{Q}_{R}-\bm{\Sigma}_{A\mathcal{F}}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{Q}_{R}-\mathbf{Q}_{L}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{F}A},

therefore, from Eq. (
229
), we obtain

(232)

[\bm{\Sigma}_{\text{eff}}]_{AA}=\bm{\Sigma}_{AA}-\bm{\Sigma}_{A\mathcal{F}}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{Q}_{R}-\mathbf{Q}_{L}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{F}A}+\mathbf{Q}_{L}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{F}}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{Q}_{R}.

Eq. (
232
) has the same structure of the self-energy (
215
) derived from Eq. (
213
). Indeed, it is easy to verify that the proper
AA
self-energy (
232
) is equal to Eq. (
215
). From Eq. (
42
), one can show that
(\mathbf{Q}_{L}\mathbf{D}^{-1})_{\mu\,\alpha\beta}^{ab}=-Q_{\mu\,\alpha\beta}\delta^{ab}
and
(\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{Q}_{R})_{\alpha\beta\,\mu}^{ab}=-Q_{\alpha\beta\,\mu}\delta^{ab}
. Now, using this in Eq. (
232
) leads to Eq. (
215
).
Consequently, it also coincides with the self-energy of the gauge field
A
in the SOYM theory.

Thus, we have shown that we can obtain the proper self-energy in the FOYM theory using two distinct approaches. Moreover, the proper self-energy of the field
A
in the FOYM formulation, obtained with these approaches, coincides with the respective self-energy computed in the SOYM formulation.

9
Gravity

In this section, we concisely review the quantum equivalence between the first- and second-order formulations of gravity. Here, we consider the case of pure gravity.
The structural identities for gravity are presented here, and their explicit verification at one-loop order is examined. We aim to extend the findings in
[
11
]
to demonstrate that, with the appropriate quantization of the FOGR theory, the first- and second-order formalism are fully equivalent at the quantum level. This equivalence is reflected in the verification of the structural identities at the integrand level, indicating that dimensional regularization is not a necessary condition for establishing equivalence. For more details, we refer the reader to Refs.
[
10
,
11
]
.

9.1
Hilbert-Palatini action

Shifting the auxiliary field in Eq. (
158
) by its classical value

(233)

G_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}=\mathring{{G}}_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}+\mathchoice{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}(h)(h^{\pi\tau}_{,\rho}-\kappa^{2}J^{\pi\tau}_{\rho})

we arrive at the equivalent generating functional

(234)

\begin{split}Z_{\text{HP}}[j,J]={}&N\int\mathop{\mathcal{D}\phi^{\mu\nu}}\mathop{\mathcal{D}d_{\nu}}\mathop{\mathcal{D}\bar{d}_{\mu}}\mathop{\mathcal{D}\mathring{{G}}_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}}|\det\mathchoice{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}(h)|^{1/2}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\times\exp i\int\mathop{d^{D}x}\left({\mathcal{L}}_{\text{eff}}^{\text{II}}+\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}{\mathring{{G}}}_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}\mathchoice{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}(h){\mathring{{G}}}_{\pi\tau}^{\rho}+\mathcal{L}_{\text{HP-src}}\right),\end{split}

where

(235)

\mathcal{L}_{\text{HP-src}}={J}_{\lambda}^{\mu\nu}\mathchoice{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}(h)h^{\pi\tau}_{,\rho}-\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2}J^{\mu\nu}_{\lambda}\mathchoice{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}{(M^{-1})^{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}_{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}}(h)J^{\pi\tau}_{\rho}+j_{\mu\nu}\phi^{\mu\nu}.

Integrating the auxiliary field
\mathring{{G}}_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}
results in the determinant
|\det\mathchoice{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}(h)|^{-1/2}
. In previous works
[
11
,
10
,
30
]
(and references within), it is argued that this determinant is trivial when using dimensional regularization.
However, as we discussed previously, these determinants can play a relevant role in the path integral formalism. Moreover, this argument is not valid at finite temperature, since tadpole-like contributions (loop integrals which are independent of external momenta), which is the case of this determinant, do not vanish. In Appendix
12
, we illustrate this with a simple example using the
\phi^{4}
theory.

Nevertheless, we have introduced the Senjanović determinant
|\allowbreak\det\mathchoice{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 4.12852pt{\rho}\kern 4.2152pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}{M^{{{\mu\nu}{\pi\tau}}}_{{{\lambda}\kern 3.60768pt{\rho}\kern 3.93353pt}}}(h)|^{+1/2}
in the measure (see Eq. (
234
